Sum rules and Simon spectral gem problem on higher order Szegő theorems
Zhihua Du

Abstract
. In this work, we give a formula for coefficients of orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle. By using this formula, a new and computable approach is provided for sum rules which applying to a spectral gem problem proposed by Barry Simon on higher order Szegő theorems.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
42C05, 35P05

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we will study orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle (usually for short, OPUC). For the background of OPUC, we refer the readers to the references
[
12
,
13
,
18
]
. Let
\mathbb{D}=\{z:|z|<1\}
be the open unit disc and
d\mu
be a probability measure with infinite support on the unit circle
\partial\mathbb{D}
in the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
, by Lebesque decomposition, we always write

d\mu(\theta)=w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}+d\mu_{s}(\theta),\,\,\,\theta\in[0,2\pi],

(1.1)

where
d\mu_{ac}=w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}
is the absolutely continuous part of
d\mu
,
d\mu_{s}
is the singular part of
d\mu
. Here
\partial\mathbb{D}
is identified with the interval
[0,2\pi]
via the map
e^{i\theta}\rightarrow\theta
.

Using Gram-Schmidt procedure, the system of monic orthogonal polynomials on the unit circle,
\{\Phi_{n}(z)\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
, is obtained by orthogonalizing the system of monomials,
\{z^{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
with respect to
d\mu
satisfying

\langle\Phi_{m},\Phi_{n}\rangle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\overline{\Phi_{m}(e^{i\theta})}\Phi_{n}(e^{i\theta})d\mu(\theta)=\kappa_{n}^{-2}\delta_{mn}

(1.2)

with
\kappa_{n}>0
, where
\delta_{mn}
is the Kronecker symbol,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the inner product on
L^{2}(d\mu)
with norm
\|\cdot\|=\sqrt{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
. Set
\varphi_{n}(z)=\kappa_{n}\Phi_{n}(z)
, then
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n=0}^{\infty}
is the system of orthonornmal polynomials on the unit circle fulfilling

\langle\varphi_{m},\varphi_{n}\rangle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\varphi_{m}(e^{i\theta})\varphi_{n}(e^{i\theta})d\mu(\theta)=\delta_{mn}.

(1.3)

Up to now, OPUC has a rich theory since Szegő introduced them one hundred years ago in his papers
[
16
,
17
]
in 1920-1921. Just as many other orthogonal polynomials, OPUC has a nice recursion relation called Szegő recursion, that is,

\Phi_{n+1}(z)=z\Phi_{n}(z)-\overline{\alpha}_{n}\Phi_{n}^{*}(z),

(1.4)

where
\alpha_{n}=-\overline{\Phi_{n+1}(0)}\in\mathbb{D}
is called Verblunsky coefficient (cf.
[
12
]
), and the reversed polynomial
\Phi_{n}^{*}(z)
of
\Phi_{n}(z)
is defined by

\Phi_{n}^{*}(z)=z^{n}\overline{\Phi_{n}(1/\overline{z})}.

(1.5)

Presumably Szegő theorem is the most remarkable result in the theory of OPUC (see
[
14
]
). By Verblunsky’s works
[
19
]
, it can be stated as a sum rule as follows

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\log(1-|\alpha_{n}|^{2}),

(1.6)

which implies that

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty\,\,\,\mbox{if and only if}\,\,\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|\alpha_{n}|^{2}<\infty.

(1.7)

In almost past twenty years, there was a great deal of investigation on sum rules for Jacobi matrices, orthogonal polynomials and Schrödinger operators, starting with the works of Deift-Killip
[
3
]
and Killip-Simon
[
10
]
and followed by many others (see
[
1
,
2
,
7
,
15
,
11
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
20
,
8
,
9
]
and therein).

The sum rules for OPUC are some analogues of the above Szegő theorem in Verblunsky form and usually called higher order Szegő theorems due to Simon
[
12
]
(Nevertheless, throughout this paper, we only call some analogues of (1.9) below higher order Szegő theorems). In his beautiful colloquium monograph
[
12
,
13
]
, the following sum rule was established

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(1-\cos\theta)\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}|\alpha_{0}+1|^{2}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(\log(1-|\alpha_{n}|^{2})+|\alpha_{n}|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|\alpha_{n+1}-\alpha_{n}|^{2}.

(1.8)

It leads to that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(1-\cos\theta)\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(|\alpha_{n+1}-\alpha_{n}|^{2}+|\alpha_{n}|^{4}\Big)<\infty,

(1.9)

where “
\Longleftrightarrow
” means “if and only if”.

(1.7) and (1.9) are spectral theory results. Such results are called “Gems” of spectral theory by Simon (see
[
14
]
). In
[
15
]
, Simon and Zlatoš obtained the following spectral results

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(1-\cos\theta)^{2}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(|\alpha_{n+2}-2\alpha_{n+1}+\alpha_{n}|^{2}+|\alpha_{n}|^{6}\Big)<\infty

(1.10)

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\big(1-\cos(\theta-\theta_{1})\big)\big(1-\cos(\theta-\theta_{2})\big)\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\,\,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\left|\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{2}\big(S-e^{-i\theta_{j}}\big)\alpha\right\}_{n}\right|^{2}+|\alpha_{n}|^{4}\right)<\infty

(1.11)

for
\theta_{1}\neq\theta_{2}
in
[0,2\pi]
, where
S
is the left shift operator given by

(S\alpha)_{n}=\alpha_{n+1}.

(1.12)

Noting the forms of all the above results, it was natural for Simon to make a general conjecture as follows

Conjecture 1.1
.

For
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{k}
distinct in
[0,2\pi)
and
m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{k}
strictly positive integers,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\big(1-\cos(\theta-\theta_{j})\big)^{m_{j}}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\,\,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\left|\left\{\prod_{j=1}^{k}\big(S-e^{-i\theta_{j}}\big)^{m_{j}}\alpha\right\}_{n}\right|^{2}+|\alpha_{n}|^{2m+2}\right)<\infty

(1.13)

in which
m=\max_{j=1,2,\ldots,k}m_{j}
.

In other words, this is to say that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\big(1-\cos(\theta-\theta_{j})\big)^{m_{j}}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty

(1.14)

if and only if

(\mathrm{S1})\hskip 99.58464pt\prod_{j=1}^{k}\big(S-e^{-i\theta_{j}}\big)^{m_{j}}\alpha\in\ell^{2}\hskip 99.58464pt

(1.15)

and

(\mathrm{S2})\hskip 113.81102pt\alpha\in\ell^{2\max_{j}(m_{j})+2}.\hskip 110.96574pt

(1.16)

However, this conjecture is always not right. For the case of
\theta_{1}=0
,
\theta_{2}=\pi
,
m_{1}=1
and
m_{2}=1
, in
[
8
]
, Lukic constructed a counterexample such that

(S-1)^{2}(S+1)\alpha\in\ell^{2}
and
\alpha\in\ell^{6}
but

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(1-\cos\theta)^{2}(1+\cos\theta)\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}=-\infty.

(1.17)

In the same paper, Lukic made an improved conjecture in place of (S1) and (S2) with the following conditions

(\mathrm{L1})\hskip 42.67912pt\alpha\,\,\mbox{can be expressed as}\,\,\alpha=\beta^{(1)}+\beta^{(2)}+\cdots\beta^{(k)},\hskip 42.67912pt

(1.18)

(\mathrm{L2})\hskip 99.58464pt\big(S-e^{-i\theta_{j}}\big)^{m_{j}}\beta^{(j)}\in\ell^{2}\hskip 96.73936pt

(1.19)

and

(\mathrm{L3})\hskip 116.6563pt\beta^{(j)}\in\ell^{2m_{j}+2}.\hskip 116.6563pt

(1.20)

Lukic conjecture is

Conjecture 1.2
.

Let
\theta_{j}
and
m_{j}
be as in Conjecture 1.1, then (1.14) is equivalent to that there exists a sequence of
\beta^{(1)},\beta^{(2)},\ldots,\beta^{(k)}
such that (1.18)-(1.20) hold.

As usual, we call the above conjectures of type (
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{k};m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{k}
) for fixed
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{k};m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{k}
. The integral in (1.13) is called higher order Szegő integral whereas the integral in (1.7) is called Szegő integral. Under appropriate conditions, partial results for these conjectures of different types have been established. For instance, Lukic gave a (
0,\pi;2,1
) result in
[
8
]
, that is

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(1-\cos\theta)^{2}(1+\cos\theta)\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,(S-1)^{2}\alpha\in\ell^{4}

(1.21)

as
(S-1)(S+1)\alpha\in\ell^{2}
and
\alpha\in\ell^{6}
.
In
[
7
]
, Golinskii and Zlatoš obtained a general result of type (
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\ldots,\theta_{k};m_{1},m_{2},\ldots,m_{k}
) in
\ell^{4}
. More precisely, suppose
\alpha\in\ell^{4}
, then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}|Q_{N}(e^{i\theta})|^{2}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\overline{Q}_{N}(S)\alpha\in\ell^{2},

(1.22)

where
Q_{N}
is any complex polynomial given by

Q_{N}(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}q_{j}z^{j}

(1.23)

in which
q_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
j=0,1,\ldots,N
, and

\overline{Q}_{N}(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}\overline{q}_{j}z^{j}=z^{n}Q^{*}_{N}(1/z).

(1.24)

For a long time, the main approach to attack these conjectures consists of three ingredients, step-by-step sum rule, constructing of positive terms and semi-continuity of entropy, which based on the relative Szegő functions introduced by Simon
[
12
]
. But there is no explanation for why this approach is valid other than good luck because it involved many clever algebraic and combinatorial manipulations for sums of positive terms. Until recent years, Gamboa, Nagel and Rouault partially changed this situation and gave partial explanation via their study on sum rules by using large deviations
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
. In
[
1
]
, Breuer, Simon and Zeitouni gave a pedagogical exposition on the approach of large deviations. By using this approach, in
[
2
]
, they recovered all the above known results due to Simon and Zlatoš, some special results of Golinskii-Zlatoš and a partial result of Lukic conjecture by showing Lukic’s conditions on Verblunsky coefficients imply the finiteness of the relevant higher order Szegő integral where Simon’s do not. Since the side involving Verblunsky coefficents are rather complicated as the order goes to higher, the analysis about it is very difficult and full of challenge. To present, many results for spectral gems are focus on lower order cases or general cases but under some additional assumptions (for the latter, see
[
9
]
).

In this paper, mainly motivated by the work
[
7
]
due to Golinskii and Zlatoš, we will provide a new and computable approach to higher order Szegő theorems and give an explanation why some of the known results should have suitable conditions in order to get the equivalence of the finiteness of higher order Szegő integral and some series in terms of Verblunsky coefficients. As a conclusion, in general, one can not obtain the equivalence results as in Conjecture 1.1-1.2 under no additional conditions for Simon gems with some few exceptions such as (1.9)-(1.11).

One innovation of our method is to provide a unified approach to get sum rules on OPUC and higher order Szegő theorems. This method can yield a lot of Simon spectral gems. However, these gems are conditional by strictly computing. Although the computaiton is involved, but the only thing is need to compute in order to obtain higher order Szegő theorems. Another innovation is to give explicit expression of sum rules. By the explicit expression and some algebraic operations, one can get various Simon gems (or higher order Szegő theorems) under different conditions. The main concrete results show that higher order Szegő theorems are always not unconditional since the conditional part or positive part (for details, see Section 4) appears in the side of Verblunsky coefficients.

2.
Coeffecients of OPUC

In
[
7
]
, Golinskii and Zlatoš obtained an expression for coefficients of OPUC in terms of Verblundsky coefficients (maybe) for the first time. In this section, we will give an alternative expression in a new form for these coefficients. One will find that the new expression is very useful in this work. Throughout this paper,
\alpha_{-1}=-1
as usual.

Let

\Phi_{n}(z)=z^{n}+a_{n,n-1}z^{n-1}+\cdots+a_{n,1}z+a_{n,0},

(2.1)

by (1.4),

a_{n,0}=-\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}.

(2.2)

For the coefficients
a_{\cdot,\cdot}
, we have the following recursion relation.

Proposition 2.1
.

For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leq m\leq n
,

\displaystyle a_{n,n-m}

\displaystyle=-\overline{\alpha}_{m-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{n-m-1}\overline{\alpha}_{j+m}\alpha_{j}-\overline{\alpha}_{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{n-m-1}\sum_{l=1}^{m-1}\beta_{k+m,k+l}a_{k+l,k},

(2.3)

in which
\beta_{s,t}=\overline{\alpha}_{s}\alpha_{t}
. As usual, the sum
\sum_{j=k}^{l}\cdot=0
as
l<k
.

Proof.

By Szegő recursion (1.4), we have

a_{n,m}=a_{n-1,m-1}-\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\overline{a_{n-1,n-1-m}}

(2.4)

for
1\leq m\leq n-1
. Replacing
n
and
m
by
n-1
and
n-1-m
, then

a_{n-1,n-1-m}=a_{n-2,n-2-m}-\overline{\alpha}_{n-2}\overline{a_{n-2,m-1}}.

(2.5)

Therefore, by substituting (2.5) into (2.4),

a_{n,m}=a_{n-1,m-1}+\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\alpha_{n-2}a_{n-2,m-1}-\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\overline{a_{n-2,n-2-m}}.

(2.6)

By induction, we get

\displaystyle a_{n,m}=

\displaystyle a_{n-1,m-1}+\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\alpha_{n-2}a_{n-2,m-1}+\cdots+\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\alpha_{m}a_{m,m-1}

\displaystyle-\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\overline{a_{m,0}}.

(2.7)

So by (2.2),

\displaystyle a_{n,m}-a_{n-1,m-1}=\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\alpha_{m-1}+\sum_{j=m}^{n-2}\beta_{n-1,j}a_{j,m-1},

(2.8)

where
\beta_{n-1,j}=\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\alpha_{j}
.

Repeating (2.8) in place of the subscript pair
(n,m)
with
(n-1,m-1),(n-2,m-2),\ldots,(n-m+1,1)
respectively, and adding them together, we obtain

a_{n,m}=a_{n-m,0}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\overline{\alpha}_{n-m+k}\alpha_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{j=k}^{n-m+k-2}\beta_{n-m+k-1,j}a_{j,k-1}.

(2.9)

Thus

\displaystyle a_{n,n-m}=

\displaystyle a_{m,0}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-m-1}\overline{\alpha}_{m+k}\alpha_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-m}\sum_{j=k}^{m+k-2}\beta_{m+k-1,j}a_{j,k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{m,0}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-m-1}\overline{\alpha}_{m+k}\alpha_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\beta_{m,j}a_{j,0}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-m}\sum_{j=k}^{m+k-2}\beta_{m+k-1,j}a_{j,k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\overline{\alpha}_{m-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{n-m-1}\overline{\alpha}_{m+j}\alpha_{j}-\overline{\alpha}_{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-m-1}\sum_{j=k+1}^{m+k-1}\beta_{m+k,j}a_{j,k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\overline{\alpha}_{m-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{n-m-1}\overline{\alpha}_{j+m}\alpha_{j}-\overline{\alpha}_{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{n-m-1}\sum_{l=1}^{m-1}\beta_{k+m,k+l}a_{k+l,k}.\qed

Remark 2.2
.

It is convenient to rewrite (2.3) as in the following form

\displaystyle a_{k_{0}+l_{0},k_{0}}=

\displaystyle-\overline{\alpha}_{l_{0}-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{k_{0}-1}\overline{\alpha}_{j+l_{0}}\alpha_{j}-\overline{\alpha}_{l_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{l_{0}-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}-1}\sum_{l=1}^{l_{0}-1}\beta_{k+l_{0},k+l}a_{k+l,k}

(2.10)

for
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
l_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
in which
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

In particular, we have

Corollary 2.3
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

a_{n,n-1}=-\overline{\alpha}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}\overline{\alpha}_{j+1}\alpha_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\overline{\alpha}_{j}\alpha_{j-1}.

(2.11)

Theorem 2.4
.

Let

G(k,l)=-\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\overline{\alpha}_{j+l}\alpha_{j}-\overline{\alpha}_{l}\sum_{j=1}^{l-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1},\,\,k,l\geq 1,

(2.12)

then for any
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
l_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle a_{k_{0}+l_{0},k_{0}}=G(k_{0},l_{0})

\displaystyle+\sum_{p=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{l_{1}=p}^{l_{0}-1}\cdots\sum_{l_{p}=1}^{l_{p-1}-1}\sum_{k_{1}=p}^{k_{0}-1}\cdots\sum_{k_{p}=1}^{k_{p-1}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}\beta_{k_{2}+l_{1},k_{2}+l_{2}}\cdots\beta_{k_{p}+l_{p-1},k_{p}+l_{p}}G(k_{p},l_{p})

\displaystyle\triangleq G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{p=1}^{l_{0}-1}\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p},l_{p}).

(2.13)

Remark 2.5
.

For convenient, in (2.13) and what follows, we always use the following notation of multiple sums

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{t_{s}=p-s+1}^{t_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}c_{t_{s}}=\sum_{t_{1}=p}^{t_{0}-1}\sum_{t_{2}=p-1}^{t_{1}-1}\cdots\sum_{t_{p}=1}^{t_{p-1}-1}c_{t_{1}}c_{t_{2}}\cdots c_{t_{p}}.

Remark 2.6
.

Interchanging the subscripts
k_{s}
and
l_{s}
, we also have

\displaystyle a_{k_{0}+l_{0},k_{0}}=G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{p=1}^{l_{0}-1}\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p},l_{p}).

(2.14)

By a convention,
\prod_{j=s}^{t}c_{j}=1
as
t<s
, (2.13) and (2.14) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle a_{k_{0}+l_{0},k_{0}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{l_{0}-1}\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p},l_{p})

(2.15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=0}^{l_{0}-1}\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p},l_{p}).

(2.16)

Observing (2.13)-(2.16), one can see that
G(k,l)
are bricks for these expressions of coefficients of OPUC in such form.

Proof.

In order to get (2.13), we take the method of induction.

By (2.10) or (2.11), we have

a_{k+1,k}=-\overline{\alpha}_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\overline{\alpha}_{j+1}\alpha_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\overline{\alpha}_{j}\alpha_{j-1}=G(k,1)

(2.17)

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. This is to say that (2.13) holds for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
l_{0}=1
.

Suppose that (2.13) holds for any
1\leq k<k_{0}
and
1\leq l<l_{0}
. In what follows, we prove it for
k=k_{0}
and
l=l_{0}
. By (2.10) and these assumptions, we have

\displaystyle a_{k_{0}+l_{0},k_{0}}=G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}a_{k_{1}+l_{1},k_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}\Big(G(k_{1},l_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{p=1}^{l_{1}-1}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p}\sum_{l_{s+1}=p-s+1}^{l_{s}-1}\right)\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p}\sum_{k_{s+1}=p-s+1}^{k_{s}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s+1}+l_{s},k_{s+1}+l_{s+1}}G(k_{p+1},l_{p+1})\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}G(k_{1},l_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{p=1}^{l_{1}-1}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p}\sum_{l_{s+1}=p-s+1}^{l_{s}-1}\right)\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p}\sum_{k_{s+1}=p-s+1}^{k_{s}-1}\right)\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s+1}+l_{s},k_{s+1}+l_{s+1}}G(k_{p+1},l_{p+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}G(k_{1},l_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{l_{1}=2}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{p=1}^{l_{1}-1}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p}\sum_{l_{s+1}=p-s+1}^{l_{s}-1}\right)\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p+1},l_{p+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}G(k_{1},l_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{p=1}^{l_{0}-2}\sum_{l_{1}=p+1}^{l_{0}-1}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p}\sum_{l_{s+1}=p-s+1}^{l_{s}-1}\right)\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p+1},l_{p+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}G(k_{1},l_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{p+1=2}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{l_{1}=p+1}^{l_{0}-1}\left(\prod_{s+1=2}^{p+1}\sum_{l_{s+1}=(p+1)-(s+1)+1}^{l_{(s+1)-1}-1}\right)\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p+1},l_{p+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}G(k_{1},l_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{p+1=2}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{l_{1}=p+1}^{l_{0}-1}\left(\prod_{s=2}^{p+1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p+1},l_{p+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}G(k_{1},l_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{p+1=2}^{l_{0}-1}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p+1},l_{p+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{k_{0}-1}\beta_{k_{1}+l_{0},k_{1}+l_{1}}G(k_{1},l_{1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{p^{\prime}=2}^{l_{0}-1}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p^{\prime}}\sum_{l_{s}=p^{\prime}-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p^{\prime}}\sum_{k_{s}=p^{\prime}-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p^{\prime}}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p^{\prime}},l_{p^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{p^{\prime}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p^{\prime}}\sum_{l_{s}=p^{\prime}-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\left(\prod_{s=1}^{p^{\prime}}\sum_{k_{s}=p^{\prime}-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p^{\prime}}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p^{\prime}},l_{p^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0},l_{0})+\sum_{p^{\prime}=1}^{l_{0}-1}\prod_{s=1}^{p^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{l_{s}=p^{\prime}-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\sum_{k_{s}=p^{\prime}-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p^{\prime}}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p^{\prime}},l_{p^{\prime}}).

In the fourth equality, we have used the usual convention that
\sum_{j=s}^{t}c_{j}=0
as
t<s
for the sums involving
l_{1}
and
k_{1}
.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

Set
k_{s+1}=k_{s}^{\prime}
and
l_{s+1}=l_{s}^{\prime}
, by assumption of induction, in the above second equality, we have

\displaystyle a_{k_{1}+l_{1},k_{1}}=a_{k_{0}^{\prime}+l_{0}^{\prime},k_{0}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{0}^{\prime},l_{0}^{\prime})+\sum_{p=1}^{l_{0}^{\prime}-1}\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}^{\prime}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}^{\prime}-1}\sum_{l_{s}^{\prime}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}^{\prime}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}^{\prime}+l_{s-1}^{\prime},k_{s}^{\prime}+l_{s}^{\prime}}G(k_{p}^{\prime},l_{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G(k_{1},l_{1})+\sum_{p=1}^{l_{1}-1}\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{l_{s+1}=p-s+1}^{l_{s}-1}\sum_{k_{s+1}=p-s+1}^{k_{s}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s+1}+l_{s},k_{s+1}+l_{s+1}}G(k_{p+1},l_{p+1}).

By (2.12) and (2.14) (or (2.16)), we further have the following expression of coefficients of OPUC in another form.

Theorem 2.8
.

For any
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
l_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle a_{k_{0}+l_{0},k_{0}}=\sum_{p=0}^{l_{0}-1}\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p}}\beta_{j+l_{p}-1,j-1},

(2.18)

where
\beta_{s,t}=\overline{\alpha}_{s}\alpha_{t}
.

Proof.

Rewrite (2.12) by

G(k,l)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\overline{\alpha}_{j+l-1}\alpha_{j-1}-\overline{\alpha}_{l}\sum_{j=1}^{l-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}.

(2.19)

In order to get (2.18), we consider two consecutive terms indexed by
p
in (2.16) and split them by using (2.19). More precisely, denote

\displaystyle\mathrm{Term}_{p}=

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}G(k_{p},l_{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p}}\beta_{j+l_{p}-1,j-1}

\displaystyle-\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\overline{\alpha}_{l_{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{l_{p}-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p}}\beta_{j+l_{p}-1,j-1}

\displaystyle-\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\overline{\alpha}_{l_{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{l_{p}-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathrm{Term}_{p,1}+\mathrm{Term}_{p,2}.

(2.21)

Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Term}_{p,2}+\mathrm{Term}_{p+1,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\overline{\alpha}_{l_{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{l_{p}-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p+1}}\beta_{j+l_{p+1}-1,j-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\overline{\alpha}_{l_{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{l_{p}-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\sum_{k_{p+1}=1}^{k_{p}-1}\sum_{l_{p+1}=1}^{l_{p}-1}\beta_{k_{p+1}+l_{p},k_{p+1}+l_{p+1}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p+1}}\beta_{j+l_{p+1}-1,j-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\overline{\alpha}_{l_{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{l_{p}-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\sum_{k_{p+1}=0}^{k_{p}-1}\sum_{l_{p+1}=1}^{l_{p}-1}\beta_{k_{p+1}+l_{p},k_{p+1}+l_{p+1}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p+1}}\beta_{j+l_{p+1}-1,j-1}

\displaystyle-\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\sum_{l_{p+1}=1}^{l_{p}-1}\beta_{l_{p},l_{p+1}}\beta_{l_{p+1}-1,-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s+1}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\overline{\alpha}_{l_{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{l_{p}-1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\sum_{k_{p+1}=0}^{k_{p}-1}\sum_{l_{p+1}=1}^{l_{p}-1}\beta_{k_{p+1}+l_{p},k_{p+1}+l_{p+1}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p+1}}\beta_{j+l_{p+1}-1,j-1}

\displaystyle+\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\overline{\alpha}_{l_{p}}\sum_{l_{p+1}=1}^{l_{p}-1}\alpha_{l_{p+1}}\overline{\alpha}_{l_{p+1}-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\sum_{k_{p+1}=0}^{k_{p}-1}\sum_{l_{p+1}=1}^{l_{p}-1}\beta_{k_{p+1}+l_{p},k_{p+1}+l_{p+1}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p+1}}\beta_{j+l_{p+1}-1,j-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=(p+1)-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=(p+1)-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p+1}\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p+1}}\beta_{j+l_{p+1}-1,j-1}.

(2.22)

In the third equality of (2.20), we have used the usual convention that
\sum_{j=s}^{t}c_{j}=0
as
t<s
for the sums involving
l_{s}
. It is similar to the second equality of (2.22) for the sums involving
k_{s}

Finally,
G(k_{p},l_{p})
in the last term of (2.16) has only the first term as in (2.19). That is, for this case of
p=l_{0}-1
,

\displaystyle G(k_{l_{0}-1},l_{l_{0}-1})=\sum_{j=0}^{k_{l_{0}-1}}\overline{\alpha}_{j}\alpha_{j-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{k_{l_{0}-1}}\overline{\alpha}_{j+l_{l_{0}-1}-1}\alpha_{j-1}

(2.23)

since
l_{l_{0}-1}=1
. Thus by (2.21)-(2.23), (2.18) immediately follows from (2.16).
∎

From (2.18), we obtain the following expression for coefficients of OPUC due to Golinskii and Zlatoš (see
[
7
]
).

Corollary 2.9
.

For any
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
l_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle a_{k_{0}+l_{0},k_{0}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum_{1}^{j}a_{l}=l_{0}\\
j,a_{l}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}<k_{0}+l_{0}\\
k_{2}<k_{1}-a_{1}\\
\cdots\\
k_{j}<k_{j-1}-a_{j-1}\end{subarray}}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{1}}\alpha_{k_{1}-a_{1}}\cdots\overline{\alpha}_{k_{j}}\alpha_{k_{j}-a_{j}}.

(2.24)

Proof.

Noting that

\beta_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}=\overline{\alpha}_{k_{s}+l_{s-1}}\alpha_{k_{s}+l_{s}}=\overline{\alpha}_{k_{s}+l_{s-1}}\alpha_{(k_{s}+l_{s-1})-(l_{s-1}-l_{s})},\,\,1\leq s\leq p,

(2.25)

\beta_{j+l_{p}-1,j-1}=\overline{\alpha}_{j+l_{p}-1}\alpha_{j-1}=\overline{\alpha}_{j+l_{p}-1}\alpha_{(j+l_{p}-1)-l_{p}},\,\,0\leq p\leq l_{0}-1

(2.26)

and

\sum_{s=1}^{p}(l_{s-1}-l_{s})+l_{p}=l_{0}

(2.27)

as well as

l_{s}\leq l_{s-1}-1,\,\,\,1\leq s\leq p,

(2.28)

thus (2.24) follows from (2.18).
∎

Remark 2.10
.

Theoretically, one can deduce (2.18) from (2.24). In fact, they are same but in different forms. Nevertheless, the derivation from (2.24) to (2.18) may not always be easy. However, in this paper, (2.18) is more useful than (2.24) to our approach.

In the end of this section, we give some interesting consequences of Theorem 2.8 about the coefficients of OPUC, Verblunsky coefficients and the moments of
\mu
although these results will not be used in this paper.

Theorem 2.11
.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
0\leq m\leq n
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{m-1}\prod_{j=m}^{n-1}\big(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\big)=-\sum_{k=m}^{n}b_{k,m}\overline{a}_{n,n-k},

(2.29)

where

b_{k,l}=(-1)^{k-l}\left|\begin{array}[]{cccccc}a_{k,k-1}&1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
a_{k,k-2}&a_{k-1,k-2}&1&\cdots&0&0\\
a_{k,k-3}&a_{k-1,k-3}&a_{k-2,k-3}&\cdots&0&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
a_{k,l+2}&a_{k-1,l+2}&a_{k-2,l+2}&\cdots&1&0\\
a_{k,l+1}&a_{k-1,l+1}&a_{k-2,l+1}&\cdots&a_{l+2,l+1}&1\\
a_{k,l}&a_{k-1,l}&a_{k-2,l}&\cdots&a_{l+2,l}&a_{l+1,l}\end{array}\right|

(2.30)

for
0\leq l<k\leq n
, and

b_{k,k}=1

(2.31)

for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof.

Let

z\Phi_{n}(z)=\Phi_{n+1}(z)-\lambda_{n}\Phi_{n}(z)-\lambda_{n-1}\Phi_{n-1}(z)-\cdots-\lambda_{1}\Phi_{1}(z)-\lambda_{0}\Phi_{0}(z),

(2.32)

by comparing with the coefficients, we have

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\lambda_{n}=a_{n+1,n}-a_{n,n-1},\\
\lambda_{n-1}=a_{n+1,n-1}-a_{n,n-2}-\lambda_{n}a_{n,n-1},\\
\lambda_{n-2}=a_{n+1,n-2}-a_{n,n-3}-\lambda_{n}a_{n,n-2}-\lambda_{n-1}a_{n-1,n-2},\\
\hskip 21.33955pt\cdots\\
\lambda_{1}=a_{n+1,1}-a_{n,0}-\lambda_{n}a_{n,1}-\lambda_{n-1}a_{n-1,1}-\cdots-\lambda_{2}a_{2,1},\\
\lambda_{0}=a_{n+1,0}-\lambda_{n}a_{n,0}-\lambda_{n-1}a_{n-1,0}-\cdots-\lambda_{2}a_{2,0}-\lambda_{1}a_{1,0}.\end{cases}

(2.33)

Denote

A_{n+1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
a_{n,n-1}&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
a_{n,n-2}&a_{n-1,n-2}&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
a_{n,1}&a_{n-1,1}&a_{n-2,1}&\cdots&a_{2,1}&0&0\\
a_{n,0}&a_{n-1,0}&a_{n-2,0}&\cdots&a_{2,0}&a_{1,0}&0\\
\end{array}\right),

\Lambda_{n+1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\lambda_{n}\\
\lambda_{n-1}\\
\lambda_{n-2}\\
\vdots\\
\lambda_{1}\\
\lambda_{0}\\
\end{array}\right)

and

\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}a_{n+1,n}-a_{n,n-1}\\
a_{n+1,n-1}-a_{n,n-2}\\
a_{n+1,n-2}-a_{n,n-3}\\
\vdots\\
a_{n+1,1}-a_{n,0}\\
a_{n+1,0}\\
\end{array}\right),

then (2.33) is equivalent to

(I_{n+1}+A_{n+1})\Lambda_{n+1}=\mathfrak{a}_{n+1},

(2.34)

where
I_{n+1}
is the identity matrix of order
n+1
.
Therefore

\Lambda_{n+1}=(I_{n+1}+A_{n+1})^{-1}\mathfrak{a}_{n+1},

(2.35)

where (by direct calculations)

\displaystyle(I_{n+1}+A_{n+1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
b_{n,n-1}&1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
b_{n,n-2}&b_{n-1,n-2}&1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
b_{n,1}&b_{n-1,1}&b_{n-2,1}&\cdots&b_{2,1}&1&0\\
b_{n,0}&b_{n-1,0}&b_{n-2,0}&\cdots&b_{2,0}&b_{1,0}&1\\
\end{array}\right)

with
b_{k,l}
given by (2.30) for
0\leq l<k\leq n
.

Applying Szegő recursion (1.4), we have

\mathfrak{a}_{n+1}=-\overline{\alpha}_{n}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\overline{a}_{n,0}\\
\overline{a}_{n,1}\\
\overline{a}_{n,2}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{a}_{n,n-1}\\
1\\
\end{array}\right).

Thus the coefficients
\lambda_{m}
in (2.32) can be expressed by the coefficients of OPUC,
\Phi_{n}
, as follows

\lambda_{m}=-\overline{\alpha}_{n}\sum_{k=m}^{n}b_{k,m}\overline{a}_{n,n-k},\,\,\,0\leq m\leq n,

(2.42)

where
b_{k,m}
are given by (2.30) and (2.31).

Since
\kappa_{n}=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\rho_{j}^{-1}
with
\rho_{j}=\big(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then

\lambda_{m}=-\kappa_{m}^{2}\langle\Phi_{m},z\Phi_{n}\rangle=-\frac{\kappa_{m}}{\kappa_{n}}\langle\varphi_{m},z\varphi_{n}\rangle=-\prod_{j=m}^{n-1}\rho_{j}\langle\varphi_{m},z\varphi_{n}\rangle.

Therefore, by Proposition 1.5.9 in
[
12
]
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{m}=\overline{\alpha}_{n}\alpha_{m-1}\prod_{j=m}^{n-1}\rho_{j}^{2}=\overline{\alpha}_{n}\alpha_{m-1}\prod_{j=m}^{n-1}\big(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\big)^{2}.

(2.43)

Hence, by (2.37) and (2.38), we have that

\displaystyle(**)\hskip 11.38109pt

\displaystyle\mathrm{If}\,\,\alpha_{n}\neq 0\,\,\mbox{for some}\,\,n\in\mathbb{N},\,\,\mathrm{then}\,\,\alpha_{m-1}\prod_{j=m}^{n-1}\big(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\big)=-\sum_{k=m}^{n}b_{k,m}\overline{a}_{n,n-k}

\displaystyle\mathrm{for}\,\,\mathrm{any}\,\,0\leq m\leq n.

For the case
\alpha_{n}=0
, we denote
\alpha_{n}=\alpha_{n}(d\mu)
,
a_{n,k}=a_{n,k}(d\mu)
and
b_{n,k}=b_{n,k}(d\mu)
. By Verblunsky theorem, we introduce another probability measure
d\mu^{\prime}
whose Verblunsky coefficitients defined by

\alpha_{j}(d\mu^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\alpha_{j}(d\mu),\,\,j\neq n,\vskip 2.84526pt\\
1,\,\,\,j=n.\end{cases}

(2.44)

Thus
d\mu
and
d\mu^{\prime}
have the same Bernstein-Szegő measure
d\mu_{n-1}
such that

\alpha_{j}(d\mu_{n-1})=\alpha_{j}(d\mu^{\prime})=\alpha_{j}(d\mu)=\alpha_{j},\,\,\,0\leq j\leq n-1.

(2.45)

By the fact
(**)
, we have

\alpha_{m-1}(d\mu^{\prime})\prod_{j=m}^{n-1}\big(1-|\alpha_{j}(d\mu^{\prime})|^{2}\big)=-\sum_{k=m}^{n}b_{k,m}(d\mu^{\prime})\overline{a}_{n,n-k}(d\mu^{\prime}),\,\,0\leq m\leq n.

(2.46)

By (2.18) (or (2.24)), (2.40) and the definitions of
a_{n,k}
and
b_{n,k}
, we obtain

\alpha_{m-1}(d\mu)\prod_{j=m}^{n-1}\big(1-|\alpha_{j}(d\mu)|^{2}\big)=-\sum_{k=m}^{n}b_{k,m}(d\mu)\overline{a}_{n,n-k}(d\mu),\,\,0\leq m\leq n.

That is, (2.29) also holds as
\alpha_{n}=0
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

By Szegő recursion and (2.32), we have

\overline{\alpha}_{n}\Phi_{n}^{*}(z)=-\lambda_{n}\Phi_{n}(z)-\lambda_{n-1}\Phi_{n-1}(z)+\cdots-\lambda_{1}\Phi_{1}(z)-\lambda_{0}\Phi_{0}(z).

(2.47)

By comparing the coefficients, one can also get (2.29) from (2.42) and Proposition 1.5.8 in
[
12
]
when
\alpha_{n}\neq 0
.

In particular, setting
m=0
, we have the following

Corollary 2.13
.

\kappa_{n}^{-2}=b_{n,0}\overline{a}_{n,0}+b_{n-1,0}\overline{a}_{n,1}+\cdots+b_{1,0}\overline{a}_{n,n-1}+b_{0,0}\overline{a}_{n,n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{k,0}\overline{a}_{n,n-k},

(2.48)

where

b_{k,0}=(-1)^{k}\left|\begin{array}[]{cccccc}a_{k,k-1}&1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
a_{k,k-2}&a_{k-1,k-2}&1&\cdots&0&0\\
a_{k,k-3}&a_{k-1,k-3}&a_{k-2,k-3}&\cdots&0&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
a_{k,2}&a_{k-1,2}&a_{k-2,2}&\cdots&1&0\\
a_{k,1}&a_{k-1,1}&a_{k-2,1}&\cdots&a_{2,1}&1\\
a_{k,0}&a_{k-1,0}&a_{k-2,0}&\cdots&a_{2,0}&a_{1,0}\end{array}\right|

(2.49)

for
1\leq k\leq n
and
b_{0,0}=1
.

Theorem 2.14
.

Assume that
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
, or equivalently

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty,

then

(1)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{k,0}\overline{a}_{n,n-k}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi};

(2.50)

(2)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{k=m}^{n}b_{k,m}\overline{a}_{n,n-k}=-\alpha_{m-1}\prod_{j=m}^{\infty}\big(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha_{m-1}\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}\big(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\big)^{-1}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi};

(2.51)

(3)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{k=m}^{n}b_{k,m}\overline{a}_{n,n-k}}{\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{k,0}\overline{a}_{n,n-k}}=-\alpha_{m-1}\prod_{j=0}^{m-1}\big(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\big)^{-1},

(2.52)

where
b_{\cdot,\cdot}
are given by (2.30) and (2.31) in which
a_{\cdot,\cdot}
is given by (2.1).

Proof.

By the assumption and Szegő theorem, (2.45) follows from (2.43), (2.46) follows from (2.29), (2.47) follows from (2.45) and (2.46).
∎

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
c_{n}
be the moments of
\mu
defined by

c_{n}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-in\theta}d\mu(\theta),

then

\displaystyle c_{n}=\sum_{l=0}^{n-1}\overline{b}_{n-1,l}\alpha_{l}\kappa_{l}^{-2}=\alpha_{n-1}\prod_{k=0}^{n-2}\big(1-|\alpha_{k}|^{2}\big)+\sum_{l=0}^{n-2}\overline{b}_{n-1,l}\alpha_{l}\kappa_{l}^{-2},\,\,n\in\mathbb{N},

(2.53)

where
b_{\cdot,\cdot}
are given by (2.30) and (2.31) in which
a_{\cdot,\cdot}
are given by (2.18) (or (2.24)).

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle C_{n+1}=I_{n+1}+A_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
a_{n,n-1}&1&0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
a_{n,n-2}&a_{n-1,n-2}&1&\cdots&0&0&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
a_{n,1}&a_{n-1,1}&a_{n-2,1}&\cdots&a_{2,1}&1&0\\
a_{n,0}&a_{n-1,0}&a_{n-2,0}&\cdots&a_{2,0}&a_{1,0}&1\\
\end{array}\right),

then the columns of
C_{n+1}
are the coefficients of
\Phi_{k}
,
0\leq k\leq n
, when
\Phi_{k}
is viewed as a polynomial of order
n
with the coefficients of
z^{l}
,
k+1\leq l\leq n
setting to be
0
. That is,

\left(z^{n}\,\,z^{n-1}\,\,\cdots\,\,z\,\,1\right)C_{n+1}=\left(\Phi_{n}(z)\,\,\Phi_{n-1}(z)\,\,\cdots\,\,\Phi_{1}(z)\,\,\Phi_{0}(z)\right)

(2.60)

or

C_{n+1}^{T}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}z^{n}\\
z^{n-1}\\
\vdots\\
z\\
1\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Phi_{n}(z)\\
\Phi_{n-1}(z)\\
\vdots\\
\Phi_{1}(z)\\
\Phi_{0}(z)\\
\end{array}\right).

(2.61)

By (2.51) and
\overline{\alpha}_{n}\kappa_{n}^{-2}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}z\Phi_{n}(z)d\mu
(see (1.5.95) in
[
12
]
), we have

C_{n+1}^{T}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\overline{c}_{n+1}\\
\overline{c}_{n}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{c}_{2}\\
\overline{c}_{1}\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\overline{\alpha}_{n}\kappa_{n}^{-2}\\
\overline{\alpha}_{n-1}\kappa_{n-1}^{-2}\\
\vdots\\
\overline{\alpha}_{1}\kappa_{1}^{-2}\\
\overline{\alpha}_{0}\\
\end{array}\right).

(2.62)

Therefore, by (2.36),

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}c_{n+1}\\
c_{n}\\
\vdots\\
c_{2}\\
c_{1}\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\overline{C_{n+1}^{T}}\right)^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{n}\kappa_{n}^{-2}\\
\alpha_{n-1}\kappa_{n-1}^{-2}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{1}\kappa_{1}^{-2}\\
\alpha_{0}\\
\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}1&\overline{b}_{n,n-1}&\overline{b}_{n,n-2}&\cdots&\overline{b}_{n,1}&\overline{b}_{n,0}\\
0&1&\overline{b}_{n-1,n-2}&\cdots&\overline{b}_{n-1,1}&\overline{b}_{n-1,0}\\
0&0&1&\cdots&0&0\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&1&\overline{b}_{1,0}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0&1\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{n}\kappa_{n}^{-2}\\
\alpha_{n-1}\kappa_{n-1}^{-2}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{1}\kappa_{1}^{-2}\\
\alpha_{0}\\
\end{array}\right).

Thus

\displaystyle c_{n}=\sum_{l=0}^{n-1}\overline{b}_{n-1,l}\alpha_{l}\kappa_{l}^{-2}=\alpha_{n-1}\prod_{k=0}^{n-2}\big(1-|\alpha_{k}|^{2}\big)+\sum_{l=0}^{n-2}\overline{b}_{n-1,l}\alpha_{l}\kappa_{l}^{-2},\,\,n\in\mathbb{N}.

∎

Remark 2.16
.

Noting (2.18) (or (2.24)), (2.48) is Verblunsky’s formula in a new form (see (1.5.53) in
[
12
]
).

3.
Logarithmic moments and an algorithm for them

In this section, we consider the moments of
\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}
when
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
which we call them logarithmic moments of
d\mu
. We will provide an algorithm to calculate them.

By Szegő theorem,
\log w\in L^{1}(d\mu)
is equivalent to
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
. Therefore, given
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
, one can define

D(z)=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{i\theta}+z}{e^{i\theta}-z}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{4\pi}\right),

(3.1)

which is called Szegő function. Moreover, we define the logarithm moments
w_{k}
of
\mu
by

w_{k}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-ik\theta}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi},\,\,\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

(3.2)

when
\log w\in L^{1}(d\mu)
. Obviously,
w_{k}
are Fourier coefficients of
\log w
. That is,
w_{k}=\widehat{\log w}(k)
in which
\widehat{}
is Fourier transform defined by

\widehat{f}(k)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-ik\theta}f(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}.

In fact,
w_{k}
are also Taylor coefficients of the logarithm of Szegő function,
\log D(z)
, as
z\in\mathbb{D}
because

\log D(z)=\frac{1}{2}w_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}w_{k}z^{k},\,\,\,z\in\mathbb{D}.

(3.3)

Besides (1.6) whose original form is as follows (see (12.3.15) in
[
18
]
)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\kappa_{n}=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{4\pi}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Theta(w)}}

(3.4)

in which

\Theta(w)=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}\right),

(3.5)

Szegő actually obtained the following complete asymptotic result (see (12.3.16) in
[
18
]
or (2.4.5) in
[
12
]
)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{n}^{*}(z)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\Phi_{n}^{*}(z)}{\|\Phi^{*}_{n}\|}=D(z)^{-1}

(3.6)

uniformly on compact sets in
\mathbb{D}
when
\log w\in L^{1}(d\mu)
.

In what follows, we always assume that
\log w\in L^{1}(d\mu)
, equivalently
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
.
As in
[
7
]
, if we expand
\sqrt{\Theta(w)}D^{-1}
as

\sqrt{\Theta(w)}D(z)^{-1}=1+d_{1}z+d_{2}z^{2}+\cdots,

(3.7)

then by (3.4), (3.6) and
\|\Phi^{*}_{n}\|=\kappa_{n}^{-1}
,

d_{m}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\overline{a}_{n,n-m}.

(3.8)

Set
k_{0}=n-m
,
l_{0}=m
and let
n\rightarrow\infty
, by Theorem 2.8 and Corollary 2.9, we have

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose that
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
, then

\displaystyle d_{m}=\sum_{p=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{1}=p-1}^{\infty}\sum_{l_{1}=p}^{m-1}\prod_{s=2}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=p-s}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\overline{\beta}_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p}}\overline{\beta}_{j+l_{p}-1,j-1}

(3.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{1}=p-1}^{\infty}\sum_{l_{1}=p}^{m-1}\alpha_{k_{1}+m}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{1}+l_{1}}\cdots\sum_{k_{p}=0}^{k_{p-1}-1}\sum_{l_{p}=1}^{l_{p-1}-1}\alpha_{k_{p}+l_{p-1}}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{p}+l_{p}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p}}\alpha_{j+l_{p}-1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

(3.10)

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
k_{0}=\infty
,
l_{0}=m
and
\beta_{s,t}=\overline{\alpha}_{s}\alpha_{t}
.

Corollary 3.2
.

Suppose that
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
, then

\displaystyle d_{m}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum_{1}^{j}a_{l}=m\\
j,a_{l}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{2}<k_{1}-a_{1}\\
\cdots\\
k_{j}<k_{j-1}-a_{j-1}\end{subarray}}\alpha_{k_{1}}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{1}-a_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{k_{j}}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{j}-a_{j}}.

(3.11)

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 3.3
.

If fact, by using the convention of
\sum_{j=s}^{t}c_{j}=0
, (3.9) and (3.10) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle d_{m}=\sum_{p=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{l_{1}=p}^{m-1}\prod_{s=2}^{p}\left(\sum_{k_{s}=0}^{k_{s-1}-1}\sum_{l_{s}=p-s+1}^{l_{s-1}-1}\right)\prod_{s=1}^{p}\overline{\beta}_{k_{s}+l_{s-1},k_{s}+l_{s}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p}}\overline{\beta}_{j+l_{p}-1,j-1}

(3.9a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{m-1}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{l_{1}=p}^{m-1}\alpha_{k_{1}+m}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{1}+l_{1}}\cdots\sum_{k_{p}=0}^{k_{p-1}-1}\sum_{l_{p}=1}^{l_{p-1}-1}\alpha_{k_{p}+l_{p-1}}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{p}+l_{p}}\sum_{j=0}^{k_{p}}\alpha_{j+l_{p}-1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1},

(3.10a)

where
k_{0}=\infty
,
l_{0}=m
and
\beta_{s,t}=\overline{\alpha}_{s}\alpha_{t}
.

From (3.3) and (3.7), we can easily get

w_{k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum_{1}^{j}b_{l}=k\\
j,l\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j}\prod_{l=1}^{j}d_{b_{l}}

(3.12)

and

d_{k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum_{1}^{j}b_{l}=k\\
j,l\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!}\prod_{l=1}^{j}w_{b_{l}}

(3.13)

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

By using (3.11) and (3.12) together with some combinatorial manipulations (for instance, Lerch’s identity), Golinskii and Zlatoš obtained a striking result about the expression for
w_{k}
as follows

w_{k}=\sum_{P\in M_{k}^{0}}N(P)\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\beta(P+l),

(3.14)

where collection
P=\{(k_{j},a_{j})\}_{j=1}^{i}
with
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
(k_{j},a_{j})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{i}a_{j}=k
,
P+l=\{(k_{j}+l,a_{j})\}_{j=1}^{i}
,
\beta(P)=\alpha_{k_{1}}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{1}-a_{1}}\cdots\alpha_{k_{i}}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{i}-a_{i}}
,
N(P)
is a constant dependent on
P
, and
M_{k}^{0}
is a set of all different collections with certain properties of points in
\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}
. The detailed information about
N(P)
and
M_{k}^{0}
can be seen in
[
7
]
which we don’t need in this work.

The thrust of (3.14) is that
w_{k}
can be expressed in term of sums with a single infinite index although
d_{k}
is expressed in term of sums with multi-fold infinite indices. Based on this result, we give a direct and computable algorithm to get
w_{k}
by using (3.10) (or (3.10a)) in Theorem 3.1. For simplicity, we only calculate
w_{1}
,
w_{2}
,
w_{3}
and
w_{4}
in this section. General results about
w_{m}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
are given in Section 5.

To do so, we need the following basic facts.

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose that
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}},\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\ell^{1}
, then

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{k}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}b_{n}.

(3.15)

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}\sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\neq l\\
0\leq k,l\leq n\end{subarray}}a_{k}b_{l}+\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}b_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}b_{l}+\sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{k}\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}a_{l}+\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}b_{k}

(3.16)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}\sum_{l=0}^{k}b_{l}+\sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{k}\sum_{l=0}^{k}a_{l}-\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}b_{k},

(3.17)

thus (3.15) follows from (3.17) by taking
n\rightarrow\infty
since
\{a_{n}\},\{b_{n}\}\in\ell^{1}
.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Suppose that
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\ell^{1}
, then

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{k}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}^{2}.

(3.18)

With these preliminaries, we calculate
w_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq 4
as
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
in what follows.

Calculation for
w_{1}
: By (3.10),

d_{1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}.

(3.19)

Therefore, by (3.12),

w_{1}=-d_{1}=-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}.

(3.20)

Calculation for
w_{2}
: By (3.10a),

\displaystyle d_{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+2}\overline{\alpha}_{k+1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}.

(3.21)

By interchanging order of sums, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+2}\overline{\alpha}_{k+1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=j}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+2}\overline{\alpha}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=j+2}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\right)^{2}-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{1}^{2}-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}.

(3.22)

By Corollary 3.5,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\frac{1}{2}d_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}

(3.23)

By (3.21)-(3.23), we obtain

\displaystyle d_{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}d_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}d_{1}^{2}.

(3.24)

By (3.12) and (3.24),

\displaystyle w_{2}=-d_{2}+\frac{1}{2}d_{1}^{2}=-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}.

(3.25)

Remark 3.6
.

Observing (3.24) and (3.25), we find

\displaystyle w_{2}=-\mbox{sums with a single infinite index in}\,\,d_{2}.

(3.26)

In particular, these sums come from the only sum with a single infinite index (i.e.,
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}
in this case) and the ones (which are not products of some sums, such as
\frac{1}{2}d_{1}^{2}
in (3.24)) obtained after by interchanging order of sums for general sums with multi-fold infinite indices in (3.10) (in this case, that is
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}
). By the result (3.14) of Golinskii and Zlatoš (for short, we call this result Golinskii-Zlatoš single index theorem later), such fact exhibiting here is exact for any
w_{m}
,
m\geq 2
. In deed, from (3.10) and (3.14), we have

Proposition 3.7
.

For any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle w_{m}=-\mbox{sums with a single infinite index in}\,\,d_{m}.

So we will deduce
w_{3}
and
w_{4}
by taking partly critical computing but not by completely calculating (similar to
w_{2}
) in what follows. The same strategy can be also applied to get all other
w_{m}
,
m\geq 5
(for the latter, see Section 5 below).

Calculation for
w_{3}
: By (3.10a),

\displaystyle d_{3}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+3}\overline{\alpha}_{k+1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+3}\overline{\alpha}_{k+2}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+3}\overline{\alpha}_{k+2}\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\alpha_{l+2}\overline{\alpha}_{l+1}\sum_{j=0}^{l}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}.

(3.27)

By interchanging order of sums, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+3}\overline{\alpha}_{k+1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+3}\overline{\alpha}_{k+2}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=j+2}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+1}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=j+3}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2d_{1}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{k+1}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j+2}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

(3.28)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k+3}\overline{\alpha}_{k+2}\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\alpha_{l+2}\overline{\alpha}_{l+1}\sum_{j=0}^{l}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{l=j+2}^{\infty}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}\sum_{k=l+2}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}\sum_{k=0}^{l+1}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{l=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{l=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}\sum_{k=0}^{l+1}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{1}^{3}-d_{1}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{l=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}+\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}\sum_{j=0}^{l+1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\right)

(3.29)

By Proposition 3.7, to get the sums with a single infinite index in

then give

, we only need consider the following sums in (3.28) and (3.29):

.

By Proposition 3.4,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j+1}\alpha_{k+1}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{j+2}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}.

(3.30)

Since (by Proposition 3.4 again)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}\sum_{k=0}^{l+1}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}

then

\displaystyle w_{3}=

\displaystyle w_{4}=

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+2\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}+2\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}\alpha_{j+1}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+3}|\alpha_{j+1}|^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}\alpha_{j}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}\alpha_{j+1}|\alpha_{j+1}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\alpha_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{3}\Big)

\displaystyle+\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+3}|\alpha_{j+2}|^{2}|\alpha_{j+1}|^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}^{2}|\alpha_{j+1}|^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}\alpha_{j+1}|\alpha_{j+1}|^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}\alpha_{j}|\alpha_{j+1}|^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}^{3}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\frac{3}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{4}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\alpha_{j}^{2}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}^{4}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{4}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+3}\rho_{j+2}^{2}\rho_{j+1}^{2}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}^{2}\rho_{j+1}^{2}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}\alpha_{j+1}\rho_{j+1}^{2}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+2}\alpha_{j}\rho_{j+1}^{2}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}^{3}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\alpha_{j}^{2}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{3}-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}^{2}\rho_{j}^{2}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}^{2}\rho_{j}^{4}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}^{4}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}^{4}.

(3.39)

Remark 3.10
.

If
m=r_{1}+r_{2}+\cdots+r_{s}
with
r_{j}\geq 1
,
1\leq j\leq s
, then
(r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{s})
is called an
s
-decomposition of
m
,
1\leq s\leq m
. Similar to the calculation of
w_{4}
, all the sums with a single infinite index in
d_{m}
come from the sums with multi-fold infinite indices in it which determined by different
s
-decompositions of
m
,
(r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{s})
,
1\leq s\leq m
, as follows

\displaystyle(-1)^{s}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k_{1}+r_{1}-1}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{1}-1}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}+r_{1}}\alpha_{k_{2}+r_{2}-1}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{2}-1}\cdots\sum_{k_{s}=0}^{k_{s-1}+r_{s-1}}\alpha_{k_{s}+r_{s}-1}\overline{\alpha}_{k_{s}-1}.

(3.40)

The same strategy of calculations is applicable to get these sums with a single infinite index for general
w_{m}
as for
w_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq 4
.

As in Remark 3.9, to get (3.36) and (3.38), we can replace (3.35) and (3.37) by expanding

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j+1}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}

and

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}\overline{\alpha}_{k-1}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{l}\overline{\alpha}_{l-1}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{m}\overline{\alpha}_{m-1}

respectively. In order to get the sums with a single infinite index from (3.40), we can apply general expansion for product of several series below.

In the end of this section and as a preliminary for some general results in Section 5, we give this general expansion. To do so, we introduce the following notions and notations.

For any two series
a=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}
and
b=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_{k}
, a contractive product of
a
and
b
is defined by

a\odot b=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}b_{k}.

(3.41)

Obviously,
a\odot b
is the diagonal part of the usual product of
a
and
b
(i.e.,
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}b_{k}
).
Set
c^{(m)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(m)}_{k}
,
1\leq m\leq n
,
\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j}\big(c^{(m)}\big)
is defined to be the sum of products of all distinct contractive products of
c^{(m)}
,
1\leq m\leq n
(i.e.,
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}\cdots\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(n)}_{k}
) for
0\leq j\leq n-1
, fulfilling that there exists at least a contractive product of
j
times as a factor and other contractive product factors of at most
j
times in its summands. For instance, as
n=3
,

\prod_{m=1}^{3}\odot_{0}\big(c^{(m)}\big)=c^{(1)}c^{(2)}c^{(3)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k},

(3.42)

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{3}\odot_{1}\big(c^{(m)}\big)=\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(2)}\big)c^{(3)}+\big(c^{(2)}\odot c^{(3)}\big)c^{(1)}+\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(3)}\big)c^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(2)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}

(3.43)

and

\prod_{m=1}^{3}\odot_{2}\big(c^{(m)}\big)=c^{(1)}\odot c^{(2)}\odot c^{(3)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}.

(3.44)

As
n=4
,

\prod_{m=1}^{4}\odot_{0}\big(c^{(m)}\big)=c^{(1)}c^{(2)}c^{(3)}c^{(4)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(4)}_{k},

(3.45)

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{4}\odot_{1}\big(c^{(m)}\big)=\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(2)}\big)c^{(3)}c^{(4)}+\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(3)}\big)c^{(2)}c^{(4)}+\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)c^{(2)}c^{(3)}

\displaystyle+\big(c^{(2)}\odot c^{(3)}\big)c^{(1)}c^{(4)}+\big(c^{(2)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)c^{(1)}c^{(3)}+\big(c^{(3)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)c^{(1)}c^{(2)}

\displaystyle+\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(2)}\big)\big(c^{(3)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)+\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(3)}\big)\big(c^{(2)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)+\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)\big(c^{(2)}\odot c^{(3)}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(2)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(4)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(4)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(4)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(2)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k},

(3.46)

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{4}\odot_{2}\big(c^{(m)}\big)=\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(2)}\odot c^{(3)}\big)c^{(4)}+\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(2)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)c^{(3)}

\displaystyle+\big(c^{(1)}\odot c^{(3)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)c^{(2)}+\big(c^{(2)}\odot c^{(3)}\odot c^{(4)}\big)c^{(1)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(4)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(3)}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}

(3.47)

and

\prod_{m=1}^{4}\odot_{3}\big(c^{(m)}\big)=c^{(1)}\odot c^{(2)}\odot c^{(3)}\odot c^{(4)}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c^{(1)}_{k}c^{(2)}_{k}c^{(3)}_{k}c^{(4)}_{k}.

(3.48)

As a convention, the contractive product of a single series is itself.

Theorem 3.11
.

Assume that
a^{(m)}=\Big\{a^{(m)}_{0},a^{(m)}_{1},a^{(m)}_{2},\ldots\Big\}\in\ell^{1}
are distinct for
1\leq m\leq n
,
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\sigma(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\sigma(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=0}^{k_{n-1}}a^{(\sigma(n))}_{k_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)

(3.49)

or

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\sigma(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\sigma(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=0}^{k_{n-1}}a^{(\sigma(n))}_{k_{n}}\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right),

(3.50)

where
S_{n}
is the standard permutation group and
\sigma=\big(\sigma(1),\sigma(2),\cdots,\sigma(n)\big)
is a permutation of the set
A_{n}=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

Remark 3.12
.

It is easy to know that

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\sigma(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\sigma(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=0}^{k_{n-1}}a^{(\sigma(n))}_{k_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=0}^{k_{n-1}}a^{(\sigma(1))}_{k_{1}}a^{(\sigma(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots a^{(\sigma(n))}_{k_{n}}

(3.51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\sum_{k_{\sigma(1)}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{\sigma(2)}=0}^{k_{\sigma(1)}}\cdots\sum_{k_{\sigma(n)}=0}^{k_{\sigma(n-1)}}a^{(1)}_{k_{\sigma(1)}}a^{(2)}_{k_{\sigma(2)}}\cdots a^{(n)}_{k_{\sigma(n)}}.

(3.52)

Proof.

We take the method of induction. It is trivial for
n=1
. By Proposition 3.4, (3.50) is just (3.15) when
n=2
. Suppose that (3.49) or (3.50) holds for
n-1
as
n>3
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right),

(3.53)

where
S_{n-1}
is the standard permutation group and
\tau=\big(\tau(1),\tau(2),\cdots,\tau(n-1)\big)
is a permutation of
\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\}
.

Thus by (3.15), (3.17), (3.51) and (3.52),

\displaystyle\prod_{m=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)=\left(\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)\right)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)\right]\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}

\displaystyle-\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)\right]\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{k}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(n)}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle-\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)\right]\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{k}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(n)}_{k}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{2}}a^{(n)}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(n)}_{k_{2}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle-\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)\right]\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{k}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(n)}_{k}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle+\cdots

\displaystyle+\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{n-1}}a^{(n)}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(n)}_{k_{2}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle-\cdots

\displaystyle-\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(n)}_{k_{2}}a^{(\tau(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n-1}=0}^{k_{n-2}}a^{(n)}_{k_{n-1}}a^{(\tau(n-1))}_{k_{n-1}}\right)

\displaystyle-\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)\right]\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\sigma(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\sigma(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=0}^{k_{n-1}}a^{(\sigma(n))}_{k_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\sum_{k_{\tau(1)}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{\tau(2)}=0}^{k_{\tau(1)}}\cdots\sum_{k_{\tau(n-1)}=0}^{k_{\tau(n-2)}}a^{(1)}_{k_{\tau(1)}}a^{(2)}_{k_{\tau(2)}}\cdots\big(a^{(n)}_{k_{\tau(s)}}a^{(s)}_{k_{\tau(s)}}\big)\cdots a^{(n-1)}_{k_{\tau(n-1)}}

\displaystyle-\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)\right]\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}.

(3.54)

By the assumption of induction, for any
1\leq s\leq n-1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in S_{n-1}}\sum_{k_{\tau(1)}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{\tau(2)}=0}^{k_{\tau(1)}}\cdots\sum_{k_{\tau(n-1)}=0}^{k_{\tau(n-2)}}a^{(1)}_{k_{\tau(1)}}a^{(2)}_{k_{\tau(2)}}\cdots\big(a^{(n)}_{k_{\tau(s)}}a^{(s)}_{k_{\tau(s)}}\big)\cdots a^{(n-1)}_{k_{\tau(n-1)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(t_{m,s}\odot a^{(m)}\right),

(3.55)

where
t_{m,s}=\begin{cases}a^{(n)},\,\,m=s,\\
\mathbf{1},\,\,\,\hskip 9.95845ptm\neq s\end{cases}

in which the sequence
\mathbf{1}=\{1,1,\ldots\}
.

Hence, in order to get (3.49), we only need justify the following equality

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)\right]\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}+\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(t_{m,s}\odot a^{(m)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right).

(3.56)

Denote

L_{\mathrm{I}}=\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(m)}_{k}\right)\right]\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}

(3.57)

and

L_{\mathrm{II}}=\sum_{s=1}^{n-1}\sum_{j=0}^{n-2}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n-1}\odot_{j}\left(t_{m,s}\odot a^{(m)}\right).

(3.58)

By the definition, for fixed
1\leq s\leq n-1
and
1\leq j\leq n-2
, general terms in
L_{\mathrm{II}}
can be classified into two kinds: one has the following form

j!\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(a^{(n)}_{k}a^{(s)}_{k}\Big)a^{(s_{1})}_{k}a^{(s_{2})}_{k}\cdots a^{(s_{j})}_{k}\times\prod_{l=1}^{n-2-j}\odot_{j}\Big(a^{(t_{l})}\Big),

(3.59)

where distinct
s_{1},\ldots,s_{j}\in A_{n}\setminus\{n,s\}
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-2-j}\in A_{n}\setminus\{n,s,s_{1},\ldots,s_{j}\}
;
The other has the forms as follows

j!\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(s^{\prime}_{1})}_{k}a^{(s^{\prime}_{2})}_{k}\cdots a^{(s^{\prime}_{j})}_{k}\times\prod_{l=1}^{n-j}\odot_{j}\Big(a^{(t^{\prime}_{l})}\Big),

(3.60)

where distinct
s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{j}\in A_{n}\setminus\{n,s\}
and
t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{n-j}\in A_{n}\setminus\{s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,s^{\prime}_{j}\}
.
Moreover, for
j=0
, general terms in
L_{\mathrm{II}}
are of the form

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(n)}_{k}a^{(s^{\prime})}_{k}\times\prod_{l=1}^{n-2}\odot_{0}\Big(a^{(t^{\prime\prime}_{l})}\Big),

(3.61)

where
s^{\prime}\in A_{n}\setminus\{n\}
and distinct
t^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime\prime}_{n-2}\in A_{n}\setminus\{n,s^{\prime}\}
.

It is easy to note that the summands in (3.59) are ones in
\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j+1}\Big(a^{(m)}\Big)
in RHS of (3.56). Fixing
s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{j}
,
we repeatedly obtain (3.59) for
j+1
times with changing
s
and
s_{\mu}
each other for
1\leq\mu\leq j
. So the coefficients of summands in (3.59) are just

(j+1)\times j!=(j+1)!
which coincided with the ones in
\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j+1}\Big(a^{(m)}\Big)
in RHS of (3.56). Thus the sum of all summands in (3.59) for index
j
and the ones in (3.60) for index
j+1
together with the ones from
(j+1)!\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j+1}\Big(a^{(m)}\Big)
in
L_{\mathrm{I}}
equals to the corresponding sum for index
j+1
in RHS of (3.56). This is to say that two sides of (3.56) are equal except the sum with only one contractive product in it (i.e., index
j=1
).

However, by the definition, it is immediate to know that the sum of the summands in (3.60) and
L_{\mathrm{I}}
for index
j=1
together with the ones in (3.61) is just the one in RHS of (3.56) (viz.,
\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{1}(a^{(m)})
).
∎

Remark 3.13
.

As above, in our approach, it is important for us to get the sums with a single infinite index in (3.49) or (3.50) for
n
different series. It is noteworthy that the number of such sums is only one and it has a nice and explicit expression from the original series
a^{(l)}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}^{(l)}
,
1\leq l\leq n
. That is,

(n-1)!a^{(1)}\odot a^{(2)}\cdots\odot a^{(n)}=(n-1)!\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}^{(1)}a_{k}^{(2)}\cdots a_{k}^{(n)}.

If
t_{1}+t_{2}+\cdots+t_{p}=n
,
t_{l}\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq l\leq p
, denote
S_{n,p}(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{p})
be the set of all distinct permutations of
n
numbers consisting of
1,2,\ldots,p
with
l
repeating
t_{l}
times,
1\leq l\leq p
. In short,
S_{n,p}(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{p})
is the set of all distinct permutations whose elements are permitted to be the same. For example,
11322
and
13221
are permutations belong to
S_{5,3}(2,2,1)
. Moreover, denote
S_{n,p}
be the set of all permutations of all
n
elements which forming
S_{n,p}(t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{p})
with permission of repetition. That is to say all elements forming
S_{n,p}
are seen to be different each other although some of them are the same. In other words,
S_{n,p}
is just
S_{n}
with the permission of repeating some elements.

By Theorem 3.11, we have

Corollary 3.14
.

Assume that
a^{(l)}=\Big\{a^{(l)}_{0},a^{(l)}_{1},a^{(l)}_{2},\ldots\Big\}\in\ell^{1}
are distinct for
1\leq l\leq p
,
l,p\in\mathbb{N}
, then for any
\tau\in S_{n,p}(t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{p})
with
t_{1}+t_{2}+\cdots+t_{p}=n
,
t_{l}\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq l\leq p
,

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{p}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(l)}_{k}\right)^{t_{l}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n,p}}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\sigma(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\sigma(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=0}^{k_{n-1}}a^{(\sigma(n))}_{k_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(m))}_{k}\right)

(3.62)

or

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n,p}(t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{p})}\left(\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}a^{(\sigma(1))}_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{k_{1}}a^{(\sigma(2))}_{k_{2}}\cdots\sum_{k_{n}=0}^{k_{n-1}}a^{(\sigma(n))}_{k_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t_{1}!t_{2}!\cdots t_{p}!}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}j!\prod_{m=1}^{n}\odot_{j}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a^{(\tau(m))}_{k}\right).

(3.63)

Proof.

The key is to think
\underbrace{l,l,\ldots,l}_{t_{l}}
as
t_{l}
different elements for
1\leq l\leq p
. Then (3.62) follows from (3.49). (3.63) follows from (3.62) by the relation between
S_{n,p}
and
S_{n,p}(t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{p})
.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

As in Remark 3.13, from the expansion of the product of
n
series among which some ones are same (exactly, from the LHS of (3.63)), the only sum with a single infinite index in it is the following

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t_{1}!t_{2}!\cdots t_{p}!}(n-1)!\underbrace{a^{(1)}\odot\cdots\odot a^{(1)}}_{t_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot\underbrace{a^{(p)}\odot\cdots\odot a^{(p)}}_{t_{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t_{1}!t_{2}!\cdots t_{p}!}(n-1)!\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(a_{k}^{(1)}\Big)^{t_{1}}\Big(a_{k}^{(2)}\Big)^{t_{2}}\cdots\Big(a_{k}^{(p)}\Big)^{t_{p}}.

4.
Sum rules and higher order Szegő theorems: Special cases

In this section, by using the expressions of
w_{m}
,
0\leq m\leq 4
, obtained in the former section, we will establish some sum rules and higher order Szegő theorems for a few of special cases. Some of which were obtained by Simon, Zlatoš and others
[
2
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
12
,
15
]
. Nevertheless, the approach here is unified and computable. In particular, some of sum rules in what follows are new and explicit.

To do so, we need the following lemmas due to Golinskii and Zlatoš as well as Breuer, Simon and Zeitouni respectively (for details, see
[
2
,
7
]
).

Lemma 4.1

(
[
7
]
)
.

Let
Q
be a complex polynomial and

Z_{Q}(\mu)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}|Q(e^{i\theta})|^{2}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi},

then

Z_{Q}(\mu)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Z_{Q}(\mu_{n}),

where
\mu_{n}
is the
n
th order Bernstein-Szegő approximation of
\mu
.

Remark 4.2
.

Assume that
Q(z)=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(z-1)
, then the above result (due to Simon) was implicitly obtained in the proof of Theorem 2.8.1 in
[
12
]
. In fact,
Z_{Q}(\mu)
in this case is just
Z_{1}(\mu)
below.

Lemma 4.3

(
[
2
]
)
.

For any
\alpha
,

-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left[\log\big(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\big)+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\frac{|\alpha_{j}|^{2m}}{m}\right]<+\infty

if and only if

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}|\alpha_{j}|^{2M+2}<+\infty.

Remark 4.4
.

As
M=1
, this result is also due to Simon (see Lemma 2.8.3 in
[
12
]
).

4.1.
First order case

At first, by using the expressions of
w_{0}
and
w_{1}
, we have

Theorem 4.5
.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(1-\cos\theta)\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}>-\infty\,\,\Longleftrightarrow\,\,\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(|\alpha_{n+1}-\alpha_{n}|^{2}+|\alpha_{n}|^{4}\Big)<\infty.

(4.1)

More precisely, for any sequence
\alpha=\{\alpha_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of Verblunsky coefficients,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}(1-\cos\theta)\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Big(\log(1-|\alpha_{n}|^{2})+|\alpha_{n}|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{n-1}|^{2}.

(4.2)

Proof.

Let

Z_{1}(\mu)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}(1-\cos\theta)\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi},

(4.3)

then

Z_{1}(\mu)=w_{0}-\mathrm{Re}(w_{1}).

(4.4)

Firstly, we suppose that
\alpha\in\ell^{2}
. Under this assumption, by the classical Szegő theorem, we have

\displaystyle w_{0}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\log w(\theta)\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\log(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2}).

(4.5)

Moreover, by (3.20), we have

\mathrm{Re}(w_{1})=-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Re}(\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1})

(4.6)

in which

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}(\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{j}\overline{\alpha}_{j-1}+\overline{\alpha}_{j}\alpha_{j-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(|\alpha_{j}|^{2}+|\alpha_{j-1}|^{2}-|\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j-1}|^{2}\big).

(4.7)

Therefore, by (4.4)-(4.7), we get

\displaystyle Z_{1}(\mu)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\log(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\big(|\alpha_{j}|^{2}+|\alpha_{j-1}|^{2}-|\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j-1}|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\Big(\log(1-|\alpha_{j}|^{2})+|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}|\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j-1}|^{2}.

(4.8)

Next, we consider general
\alpha
. By Lemma 4.1, we have

Z_{1}(\mu)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Z_{1}(\mu_{n}),

(4.9)

It is well known that if the Verblunsky coefficients of
\mu
are
\{-1,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},\alpha_{n+1},\ldots\}
, then the Verblunsky coefficients of
\mu_{n}
are
\{-1,\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n},0,\ldots\}
. For any
\mu
, the Verblunsky coefficients of
\mu_{n}
is in
\ell^{2}
. By the above result of
\ell^{2}
